Figure 9
:
Left: a free boundary plane partition
\pi
of length 5, with
\pi_{1,1}=10,\pi_{4,2}=5,\dots
, corresponding to interlacing partitions
\emptyset\prec(6)\prec(7,3)\prec(9,5,2)\prec(9,7,3,1)\prec(10,8,6,2,1)
; middle: a stack of cubes depicting
\pi
via the heights of the horizontal lozenges; right: the corresponding symmetric plane partition with base contained in a
5\times 5
square.

Let us fix a real parameter
0<q<1
. We will study the asymptotics of free boundary plane partitions weighted according to their volume; that is, distributed as

\displaystyle\Prob(\pi)\propto q^{|\pi|}

(5.1)

in the limit
q\to 1
and
N\to\infty
.

Free boundary plane partitions
\pi
of length
N
weighted by their volume are in bijection with the H-ascending Schur process on

\displaystyle\vec{\lambda}=(\emptyset\prec\lambda^{(1)}\prec\dots\prec\lambda^{(N)})

(5.2)

with parameters
x_{i}=q^{N+1-i}
(the free boundary is
\lambda^{(N)}
) via the following identification:

\displaystyle\lambda^{(i)}_{k}=\pi_{N-i+k,k},\ 1\leq k\leq i.

(5.3)

In this setting we have

\displaystyle\Prob(\vec{\lambda})\propto q^{\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}|\lambda^{(i)}|}.

(5.4)

The sequence of partitions
\vec{\lambda}
, itself induced by
\pi
, gives a point process via the identification
\lambda^{(i)}\mapsto\{k^{(i)}_{s}=\lambda^{(i)}_{s}-s+\frac{1}{2}\}
. It turns out the computations and formulas are simpler if we reverse time. We introduce a new sequence of partitions
\mu^{(i)}
defined by
\mu^{(i)}=\lambda^{(N-i)}
so that our Schur process becomes
\emptyset=\mu^{(N)}\prec\dots\prec\mu^{(0)}
. Proposition
2.6
becomes the following.

Theorem 5.1
.

The point process induced by
\pi
via the sequence of partitions
\mu^{(i)}
is pfaffian with
2\times 2
correlation kernel given by

K(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}K_{1,1}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})&K_{1,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})\\
-K_{1,2}(i^{\prime},k^{\prime};i,k)&K_{2,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})\end{array}\right]

(5.5)

where

\displaystyle K_{1,1}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}F(N-i,z)F(N-i^{\prime},w)\frac{1}{z^{k+1}w^{k^{\prime}+1}}\frac{\sqrt{zw}(z-w)}{(z+1)(w+1)(zw-1)}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}z,

\displaystyle K_{1,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}\frac{F(N-i,z)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}\frac{w^{k^{\prime}-1}}{z^{k+1}}\frac{\sqrt{zw}(zw-1)}{(z+1)(w-1)(z-w)}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}z,

(5.6)

\displaystyle K_{2,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}\frac{1}{F(N-i,z)F(N-i^{\prime},w)}z^{k-1}w^{k^{\prime}-1}\frac{\sqrt{zw}(z-w)}{(z-1)(w-1)(zw-1)}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}z,

the contours are counterclockwise oriented circles centered at the origin of radii slightly larger than 1 with the additional constraint that for
K_{1,2}

C_{z}
surrounds
C_{w}
if and only if
i^{\prime}\leq i
and finally where we have denoted

\displaystyle F(N-i,z):=\frac{(q/z;q)_{N}}{(q^{i+1}z;q)_{N-i}}.

(5.7)

Remark 5.2
.

Every particle in our process corresponds to a
horizontal
lozenge in the middle picture from Figure
9
, and we imagine tiling the floor of the room with infinitely many horizontal lozenges going down. Some authors (e.g.,
[
OR03
]
) prefer to write the kernel in terms of heights
h
of these lozenges. For a given particle at position
(i,k)
, the coordinate of the corresponding lozenge (in the axes depicted, with the origin at the hidden corner of the box) is
(i,h)=(i,k-\frac{i}{2})
. We opt to work throughout with the ordinate
k
of particle positions, but the reader interested in the lozenge picture has only to keep in mind
k=h+\frac{i}{2}.

We now pick a real number
\mathpzc{a}>0
. We zoom in around a point
(i,k)
in the point process induced by
\mu^{(i)}
for large
N,i,k
as
q
approaches 1. Precisely, we are interested in the asymptotic regime
r\to 0+
with

q=e^{-r}\to 1,\qquad rN\to\mathpzc{a},\qquad ri\to\mathpzc{x},\qquad rk\to\mathpzc{y}.

(5.8)

The coordinate system
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
represents our macroscopic coordinates, with
\mathpzc{y}\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq\mathpzc{x}\leq\mathpzc{a}
. The positive real number
\mathpzc{a}
plays the role of a boundary parameter. We furthermore introduce macroscopic exponential coordinates (and exponential boundary parameter)

\displaystyle\mathpzc{X}=\exp(-\mathpzc{x}),\qquad\mathpzc{Y}=\exp(-\mathpzc{y}),\qquad\mathpzc{A}=\exp(-\mathpzc{a}).

(5.9)

Notice
0<\mathpzc{A}<1,\mathpzc{A}\leq\mathpzc{X}\leq 1
. We first focus on the case
0<\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{a}
and thus
\mathpzc{A}<\mathpzc{X}<1
.

Analyzing the kernel asymptotically in the regime (
5.8
) (following
[
OR03
]
) can be reduced (more details will be given below) to the analysis of the
z
integrand
F(N-i,z)/z^{k+1/2}
.

We first note in the limit
r\to 0+
, the zeros of
F(N-i,z)
accumulate in the set
\mathcal{Z}=[0,1]
while the poles in the set
\mathcal{P}=[\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}},\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}]
. Moreover as
r\to 0+
we have by (
B.7
)

\displaystyle\frac{F(N-i,z)}{z^{k+1/2}}\sim\exp\frac{1}{r}S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})

(5.10)

where
S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\mathpzc{A}/z)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1/z)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\mathpzc{X}z)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\mathpzc{A}z)-y\log z.

To apply the method of steepest descent to
S
, we look for its critical points: that is, solutions of
z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=0
. There are two of them:

\displaystyle z_{\pm}=z_{\pm}(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=\frac{1+\mathpzc{X}-(1+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}\pm\sqrt{D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})}}{2(\mathpzc{X}-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y})}

(5.11)

where

\begin{split}D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})&=-4(1-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y})(\mathpzc{X}-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y})+(-1-\mathpzc{X}+\mathpzc{Y}+\mathpzc{A}^{2}\mathpzc{Y})^{2}\\
&=(\mathpzc{A}^{2}-1)^{2}\mathpzc{Y}^{2}-2(\mathpzc{A}-1)^{2}\mathpzc{X}\mathpzc{Y}+\mathpzc{X}^{2}-2(\mathpzc{A}-1)^{2}\mathpzc{Y}-2\mathpzc{X}+1.\end{split}

(5.12)

Remark 5.3
.

The equation
D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})=0
, the locus where
S
has a real double critical point, is a conic in the
(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})
plane. Its discriminant is
-16\mathpzc{A}(\mathpzc{A}-1)^{2}<0
and so it is the equation of an ellipse. When
\mathpzc{A}=0
(equivalently,
\mathpzc{a}\to\infty
), it becomes a parabola.

Remark 5.4
.

The two critical points in equation (
5.11
) have a singularity on the line
\mathpzc{X}=\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}
, where one of them becomes
\pm\infty
(depending on the sign of
\mathpzc{X}-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}
) while the other stays finite at
\frac{\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}-1}{(1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}-1}
as can be easily verified by an application of l’Hôpital’s rule.

Keeping
\mathpzc{x}
and hence
\mathpzc{X}
fixed, we solve for
\mathpzc{Y}
(and hence for
\mathpzc{y}
) from
D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})=0
to obtain two solutions:

\displaystyle\mathpzc{Y}_{\pm}=\frac{(1+\mathpzc{X})(1-\mathpzc{A})^{2}\pm 2(1-\mathpzc{A})\sqrt{-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{X}^{2}+\mathpzc{X}(1+\mathpzc{A}^{2})-\mathpzc{A}}}{(1-\mathpzc{A}^{2})^{2}},\qquad\mathpzc{y}_{\mp}=-\log(\mathpzc{Y}_{\pm}).

(5.13)

We note
\mathpzc{y}_{-}\leq\mathpzc{y}_{+}
and both depend on
\mathpzc{x}
; when
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a}
we have
\mathpzc{y}_{-}=\mathpzc{y}_{+}=\log(1+\mathpzc{A})
; when
\mathpzc{x}=0
(hence
\mathpzc{X}=1
) we have
\mathpzc{y}_{+}=\infty
(as
\mathpzc{Y}_{-}=0
) and
\mathpzc{y}_{-}=-\log\frac{4}{(1+\mathpzc{A})^{2}}
.

Figure 10
:
Left: a portion of the arctic curve for
\mathpzc{a}=1
in the lozenge
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})=(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y}-\mathpzc{x}/2)
coordinates; the vertical dashed line is of abscissa
\mathpzc{x}=1/2
and intersects the arctic curve in the two points
\mathpzc{h}_{\pm}=\mathpzc{y}_{\pm}-\mathpzc{x}/2
; the points of tangency of the arctic curve to the boundary of the domain on the bottom and right are also pictured. Right: a simulation (after symmetrization) of a boxed plane partition with base of size
100\times 100
and
q\approx 0.98
using the algorithm from
[
BBB+18
]
.

We call the curve

\mathcal{C}=\{(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathpzc{a}\geq\mathpzc{x}\geq 0,D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})=0\}

(5.14)

the
arctic curve
. It is depicted in Figure
10
. We denote by
\mathcal{L}
— which we call
the liquid region
, the inside of the arctic curve; that is, the domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\{(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\mathpzc{a}\geq\mathpzc{x}\geq 0,\mathpzc{y}_{-}\leq\mathpzc{y}\leq\mathpzc{y}_{+}\}.

(5.15)

We now discuss what happens to the critical points at various
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
positions in the plane. To do that, we first take care of the singularity on the line
\mathpzc{X}=\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}
or, equivalently, the line
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a}+\mathpzc{y}
. The curve
\mathcal{C}
is tangent to this line, at the tangency points
(\mathpzc{x}_{T},\mathpzc{y}_{T})
given by:

(\mathpzc{X}_{T},\mathpzc{Y}_{T})=\left(\frac{\mathpzc{A}}{1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2}},\frac{1}{1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2}}\right),\qquad(\mathpzc{x}_{T},\mathpzc{y}_{T})=(-\log\mathpzc{X}_{T},-\log\mathpzc{Y}_{T})

(5.16)

where by construction
0<\mathpzc{x}_{T}<\mathpzc{a},\mathpzc{y}_{T}=\mathpzc{x}_{T}-\mathpzc{a}<0
. We have three cases:

•

when
D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})=0
and thus
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathcal{C}
,
S
has a double critical point:
z=\frac{1+\mathpzc{X}-(1+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}}{2(\mathpzc{X}-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y})}
. This happens twice for fixed
\mathpzc{x}
: when
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{-}
or
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{+}
(the two cases coinciding at
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a},\mathpzc{y}_{-}=\mathpzc{y}_{+}=\log(1+\mathpzc{A})
). First, the case
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{+}>0
. We have
z\in(1,\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}})
. When
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\to(0,\infty)
along
\mathcal{C}
,
z\to 1
from above (so
z>1
always). When
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\to(\mathpzc{a},\log(1+\mathpzc{A}))
from above along
\mathcal{C}
,
z\to\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}
from below.
\mathcal{C}
is tangent at
(\mathpzc{a},\log(1+\mathpzc{A}))
to the line
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a}
. Second, the case
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{-}\leq\log(1+\mathpzc{A})
. If
0\leq\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
,
z\in(-\infty,-1]
with
z=-1
at
\mathpzc{x}=0
and
z\to-\infty
when
\mathpzc{x}\to\mathpzc{x}_{T}
from below. When
\mathpzc{x}\in(\mathpzc{x}_{T},\mathpzc{a}]
,
z\in[\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}},\infty)
with
z\to\infty
when
\mathpzc{x}\to\mathpzc{x}_{T}
from above and
z\to\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}
from below as
\mathpzc{x}\to\mathpzc{a}
;

•

when
D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})<0
, which means
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
is in the interior of
\mathcal{L}
, the two distinct critical points are complex conjugate with arguments
\pm\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
where we take
\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in(0,\pi)
.
\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
varies from 0 to
\pi
as
\mathpzc{y}
descends from
\mathpzc{y}_{+}
to
\mathpzc{y}_{-}
through the liquid region
\mathcal{L}
for fixed
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
. In the case
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
both double critical points
z^{t}
corresponding to
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y}_{+})
and
z^{b}
corresponding to
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y}_{-})
are positive, with
0<z^{t}<z^{b}
, so if one recenters the complex plane at any real number between
z^{t}
and
z^{b}
and considers the arguments of the two complex conjugate critical points corresponding to
\mathpzc{y}_{-}<\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{+}
, we have a similar situation as above. See Figure
11
.

•

when
D(\mathpzc{X},\mathpzc{Y})>0
(and thus for
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
not in
\mathcal{L}
) there are two distinct real critical points. We study what happens for fixed
\mathpzc{x}
. If
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+}
,
1<z_{-}<z_{+}<\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}}
with
z_{-}=1,z_{+}=\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}}
in the limit
\mathpzc{y}\to\infty
and
z_{\pm}
converging to the unique double critical point in the limit
y\to\mathpzc{y}_{+}
. If
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
, the situation is more complicated due to the singularity at
\mathpzc{X}=\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}
(equivalently on the line
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a}+\mathpzc{y}
). We again distinguish two cases. First,
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
fixed. For
\mathpzc{y}\in(\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a},\mathpzc{y}_{-})
, if
\mathpzc{y}\to y_{-}
, the two roots
z_{\pm}
converge to the corresponding (negative) real double critical point. When
\mathpzc{y}\to\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
from above,
z_{-}\to-\infty
and
z_{+}\to\frac{\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}-1}{(1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}-1}<0
from below. When
\mathpzc{y}
passes below the line
\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
and goes to
-\infty
,
z_{-}
goes from
\infty
(for
\mathpzc{y}
just below the line
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
) to
\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}
(at
\mathpzc{y}\to-\infty
) while
z_{+}
goes from
\frac{\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}-1}{(1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}-1}
at
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
to
\mathpzc{A}
at
\mathpzc{y}\to-\infty.
Second, the case
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
.
z_{-}
goes from the double critical point
z_{c}>\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}
to
\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}}
as
\mathpzc{y}
goes from
\mathpzc{y}_{-}
to
-\infty
, passing through
z_{-}=\frac{\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y}-1}{(1-\mathpzc{A}+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}-1}
at the line
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
.
z_{+}
goes from
z_{c}
to
\infty
as
\mathpzc{y}
moves from
\mathpzc{y}_{-}
and approaches the line
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
from above, and then jumps and goes from
\infty
to
\mathpzc{A}
as
\mathpzc{y}
descends from just below the line
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
to
-\infty
.

Figure 11
:
The critical points of
S
, for
\mathpzc{a}=2
and for
\mathpzc{x}=1<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
(left),
\mathpzc{x}=1.97>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
(right) fixed, as
\mathpzc{y}
varies from just above
\mathpzc{y}_{+}
to just below
\mathpzc{y}_{-}
; they are distinct real for
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+}
(denoted by an X) approaching the double critical point at
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{+}
; they are complex conjugate for
\mathpzc{y}_{+}>\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{-}
approaching the double critical point at
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{y}_{-}
and they become distinct real again (denoted by Y) diverging from said double critical point as
\mathpzc{y}
decreases below
\mathpzc{y}_{-}
.

Remark 5.5
.

In light of the above discussion, the curve
\mathcal{C}
is the locus in the
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
plane where
S
has a double critical point. Given a point
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathcal{C}
, one computes the corresponding double critical point
z_{c}(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=\frac{1+\mathpzc{X}-(1+\mathpzc{A}^{2})\mathpzc{Y}}{2(\mathpzc{X}-\mathpzc{A}\mathpzc{Y})}
. Importantly, one can go backwards as well and for each double critical point
z_{c}
, one can compute the corresponding
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=(\mathpzc{x}(z_{c}),\mathpzc{y}(z_{c}))
by solving for
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
in the following system of two equations

\displaystyle\left(z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)S(z_{c};\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=0,\qquad\left(z\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\right)^{2}S(z_{c};\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=0

(5.17)

and in doing so one obtains a
time/rational parametrization
of
\mathcal{C}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=\{(\mathpzc{x}(z_{c}),\mathpzc{y}(z_{c})):z_{c}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash(-1,1]\}

(5.18)

with the time parameter played by the double critical point of
S
. In brief
z_{c}\mapsto(\mathpzc{x}(z_{c}),\mathpzc{y}(z_{c}))
takes
\mathbb{R}\backslash(-1,1]
to
\mathcal{C}
: as
z_{c}
increases from
1+
to
\infty
we draw the upper part of
\mathcal{C}
from
(0,\infty)
to
(\mathpzc{x}_{T},\mathpzc{y}_{T})
, and then as
z_{c}
increases from
-\infty
to
-1
we draw the lower part of
\mathcal{C}
from
(\mathpzc{x}_{T},\mathpzc{y}_{T})
to
(0,-\log\frac{4}{(1+\mathpzc{A})^{2}})
.

Fix a point
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
in the plane, with
0\leq\mathpzc{x}\leq\mathpzc{a}
and
\mathpzc{y}\geq\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
, and let
z_{\pm}
be the corresponding critical points. In the asymptotic analysis that follows, the existence of a closed contour
C_{0}
on which
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{0}))=0
(where
z_{0}
is one of the two critical points), and which passes to the right of
1
(but the left of
\exp(\mathpzc{x})=\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}}
) and to the left of
0
(thus encompassing the cut on the interval
[0,1]
), will be the key ingredient in the proof. Such a contour, for
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
in the critical region and thus for complex conjugate critical points (
z_{0}=z_{+}
in that case though the choice makes no difference as
S(\overline{z})=\overline{S(z)}
), is depicted in Figure
12
(top left). For its existence, we argue as follows. We have the following limits at 0 and
-\infty
(along the real axis, say):

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to 0-}\Re S(z)=\infty,\qquad\lim_{z\to-\infty}\Re S(z)=-\infty

(5.19)

and so by the intermediate value theorem there will be a point
z
on the negative real axis with
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{0}))=0
. Likewise on the interval
(1,\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}})

\Re(S(z)-S(z_{0}))
changes sign and we thus have a point
z
in this interval with
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{0}))=0
. Connecting the contours in the upper and lower half-planes will yield the desired
C_{0}
. For a precise technical description of this argument see Lemma 6.4 in
[
Bor07
]
and note that the
f
in that statement is exactly our
S
without the log term. For
\mathpzc{x}=0
the preceding discussion becomes simpler. In this case, for
z
on the unit circle
\{|z|=1\}
, we have, by direct computation,
2\Re(S(z))=S(z)+S(1/z)=0
. Moreover in the case of complex conjugate critical points
z_{\pm}
we have
|z_{\pm}|=1
and so the
C_{0}
is just the unit circle.

We finally remark that in the case
a\to\infty
the above argument simplifies considerably and
C_{0}
is just the circle around the origin of radius
\exp(\mathpzc{x}/2)
.

Before stating our first asymptotic result, we fix a few useful notations. We concentrate on the case
0<\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, but the case
\mathpzc{x}_{T}<\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{a}
can be treated similarly. We will study the case
\mathpzc{x}=0
separately.

For
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathcal{L}
, we denote by
\gamma_{+}
any simple counter-clockwise contour (path) joining the two corresponding critical points
z_{+}
and
z_{-}
just to the right of 1. It depends of course on
\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
which is the argument of
z_{+}
. By
\gamma_{-}
we denote a clockwise contour (path) joining the same two points but which passes to the left of 0. We state the result for
i^{\prime}\leq i
. For
i>i^{\prime}
the only difference is one replaces
\gamma_{+}
by
\gamma_{-}
.

Finally, one only needs to look in the half-space
\mathpzc{y}\geq\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
. The reason for this is combinatorial: below the line
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
we will see only particles, as the partition
\mu^{(N-i)}
has at most
i
parts due to the interlacing constraints. Therefore the kernel will not be of interest around points
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
in the half-space given by
y<\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in(0,\mathpzc{a})\times\mathbb{R}
and
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
. Let

(i,i^{\prime})=\left(\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{i},\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{i}^{\prime}\right),\qquad(k,k^{\prime})=\left(\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k},\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\right)

(5.20)

where
\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{i}\in\mathbb{N},\mathsf{k},\mathsf{k}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
are fixed. Then
ri,ri^{\prime}\to\mathpzc{x}
and
rk,rk^{\prime}\to\mathpzc{y}
as
r\to 0+
. When
rN\to\mathpzc{a}
we find that

\lim_{r\to 0+}K_{1,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+},\\
\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C}(1-e^{-\mathpzc{x}}z)^{\mathsf{i}-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}z^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\mathrm{d}z,&\text{otherwise }\end{cases}

(5.21)

where
C
is

•

\gamma_{\pm}
if
\mathpzc{y}_{-}\leq y\leq\mathpzc{y}_{+}
(
\gamma_{+}
if and only if
\mathsf{i}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{i}
);

•

a positively oriented circle of radius
1+\epsilon
centered at the origin for some
0<\epsilon\ll 1
if
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
and
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
;

•

a positively oriented circle containing the cut
\mathcal{P}
but passing to the right of 1 if
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
and
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
.

Proof.

We will give a similar argument to the one given in Section 3.1 of
[
OR03
]
. The interested reader will note the same argument has been applied before (modulo notation, conventions, and some minor technical details), in various related models (for both normal and/or strict as opposed to symmetric/free boundary plane partitions — see
[
OR03
,
Vul07
,
FS03
,
BMRT12
]
) and the analysis carries over almost mutatis mutandis.

Throughout the proof we restrict to the case
\mathsf{i}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{i}
, in which case the
z
contour is on the outside in the double contour integral formula for
K_{1,2}
. The other case follows similarly. We also write
S(z)
in lieu of
S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
whenever possible.

The idea is that we deform the original integration contours for
K_{1,2}
around the complex plane and have them pass through the critical point
z_{+}
(and in the case of complex conjugate critical points, both
z_{+}
and
z_{-}
). We want to make
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))
negative everywhere except at
z_{+}
(or
z_{\pm}
in the complex conjugate case), while making
\Re(S(w)-S(z_{+}))
positive everywhere except
z_{+}
(respectively
z_{\pm}
). We then observe
S(z)-S(w)=(S(z)-S(z_{+}))-(S(w)-S(z_{+}))
and employ multiple times the following simple limit:

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}\exp\left(\frac{1}{r}f(z)\right)\mathrm{d}z\to 0,\ r\to 0+

(5.22)

if
f
is smooth and
\Re f<0
for all but finitely many points along the simple closed contour
\gamma
.

In the four panels of Figure
12
we illustrate, for various situations that will arise, the level lines of
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))
. The first three figures correspond to points
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
with
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, sitting above the arctic curve, inside the liquid region
\mathcal{L}
, and below the arctic curve but above the line
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{y}+\mathpzc{a}
respectively. The last corresponds to
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
below the arctic curve but with
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, the only case that needs special treatment when
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
.

K_{1,2}
is explicitly given in Theorem
5.1
. In the limit, the integrand
\frac{F(N-i,z)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}\frac{w^{k^{\prime}+1}}{z^{k+1}}
is approximated by

\displaystyle\exp\frac{1}{r}\left(S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})-S(w;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\right)

(5.23)

and it is this function that will provide the dominant asymptotic contribution.

Throughout the proof, contours of integration for
z
and
w
will move around. One has to take care that the
z
contour never crosses the interval
[\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}},\frac{1}{A}]
where the poles of the function
F
accumulate in the limit (but the
w
contour can certainly cross this interval), and that the
w
contour never crosses the interval
[0,1]
where the zeros of the function
F
(so poles in the
w
variable) accumulate in the limit (and again, the
z
contour is of course allowed to cross said interval). None of the operations described below move the
z
or
w
contours in a way that their respective forbidden intervals are crossed, as can be explicitly checked case by case.

First, the case
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+},\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
. We deform the contours so that the
z
contour, which is on the outside, passes through
z_{+}\in(1,\exp(\mathpzc{x}))
at an angle orthogonal to the real axis — which is locally the direction of steepest descent for
\Re S
, and otherwise contains the contour
C_{0}
where
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))=0
(to ensure everywhere else
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))<0
). Similarly we deform the
w
contour so that it is contained in
C_{0}
(so that
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))>0
) and passes through
z_{+}
parallel to the real axis. See Figure
12
(top right). We observe that the factor
1/(z-w)
does not cause problems as it is integrable: we can bound
\int\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w}{z-w}
by the converging
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\frac{\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}}=8\delta\log(1+\sqrt{2})
for some small positive real
\delta
. We conclude the integral decays exponentially fast to 0 in the limit
r\to 0+
.

Second, the case
\mathpzc{y}\in(\mathpzc{y}_{-},\mathpzc{y}_{+}),\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, which is to say
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\in\mathcal{L}
. We proceed as before by passing both contours through the two critical points
z_{+}
and
z_{-}
, so that they intersect orthogonally at both. Based on the gradient of
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))
the final contours look like in Figure
12
(top left). We note that for this to be possible, we have to pass the
w
contour to the outside of the
z
contour on an arc passing through the left of 1 and connecting
z_{+}
with
z_{-}
(this arc is
\gamma_{+}
). Doing so we pick up the residue at
z=w
for every
z
between
z_{+}
and
z_{-}
along
\gamma_{+}
. The total contribution is finite and equal to

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{+}}Res_{z\to w}\left(\frac{F(N-i,z)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}w^{k^{\prime}-1/2}z^{-k-1/2}\frac{zw-1}{(z+1)(w-1)(z-w)}\right)\mathrm{d}z=\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{+}}\frac{F(N-i,w)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}w^{k^{\prime}-k+1}\mathrm{d}w\to\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{+}}(1-e^{-\mathpzc{x}}w)^{\mathsf{i}-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}w^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\mathrm{d}w,\ r\to 0+\end{split}

(5.24)

where to take the limit we have used the estimates from Appendix
B
. We now argue that the integral on the remaining contours vanishes in the limit. The remaining integrals will converge to 0 exponentially again using the basic fact from (
5.22
). Again the denominator factor
1/(z-w)
poses no further issues.

The situation repeats for a third time, for
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-},\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a},\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
. We pass both contours through
z_{+}<0
, but now as in the previous case the gradient of
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))
forces the
w
contour on the outside and the
z
on the inside, the
z
contour passing orthogonally to the real axis while the
w
contour passing parallel to it at
z_{+}
. See Figure
12
(bottom left). As we have to pass the whole
w
contour to the outside, we pick up a residue along the line
z=w
over a whole closed contour passing through
z_{+}
and to the left of 1 of contribution:

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C}Res_{z\to w}\left(\frac{F(N-i,z)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}w^{k^{\prime}-1/2}z^{-k-1/2}\frac{zw-1}{(z+1)(w-1)(z-w)}\right)\mathrm{d}z=\\
&\qquad=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C}\frac{F(N-i,w)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}w^{k^{\prime}-k+1}\mathrm{d}w\to\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C}(1-e^{-\mathpzc{x}}w)^{\mathsf{i}-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}w^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\mathrm{d}w,\ r\to 0+\end{split}

(5.25)

where again we have used Appendix
B
. The remaining integrals, as before, converge exponentially fast to 0 in the limit.

For
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, the two cases
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+}
(above the arctic curve) and
\mathpzc{y}\in(\mathpzc{y}_{-},\mathpzc{y}_{+})
(inside the liquid region) are handled using the same contours as if it were in the regime
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
. For the third case
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
, the situation is somewhat different as now the critical points
z_{\pm}
are in the interval
(\exp(\mathpzc{a}),\infty)
. We pass both contours through
z_{+}
as before. Since the
z
contour cannot cross
\mathcal{P}
, we enlarge it on the left side and have it pass through infinity until it comes back on the other side and encircles
\mathcal{P}
. We can do this as there is no residue at
\infty
in the
z
variable. Locally at
z_{+}
the final contour
C_{z}
is parallel to the axis. We now inflate the contour
C_{w}
, passing it over
C_{z}
and picking up the stated residue over the desired contour
C
, so that it goes through
z_{+}
locally perpendicular to the real axis. See Figure
12
(bottom right). Further note that if
\mathsf{i}=\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}=\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
(the diagonal of the kernel), the residue integral over
C
is 0, since the origin is no longer inside
C
.
∎

Figure 12
:
The curves (solid)
\Re(S(z)-S(z_{+}))=0
with arrows in the direction of ascent and the final
z
and
w
contours from the proof of Theorem
5.6
. Top left:
z_{+}
is one of the two complex conjugate critical points of
S
corresponding to the liquid region; top right:
z_{+}
is real corresponding to the region above the arctic curve; bottom left:
z_{+}
is real and corresponds to the region below the arctic curve and
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
; bottom right: like bottom left but with
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
.

Remark 5.7
.

We mention one qualitative difference between the case
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
handled in Figure
12
(top left, top right, bottom left) and the case
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
. If
\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}<\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
, asymptotically around
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
(below the arctic curve), one sees only particles if
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, while for
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
one sees only holes. Both regions are frozen, but in different ways. See Figure
10
for a numerical visualization of this.

Remark 5.8
.

Up to a change of variables
z\mapsto e^{-\mathpzc{x}}z
which introduces extra factors of the form
e^{\mathpzc{x}(\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k})}
that nevertheless cancel in any pfaffian computation (as they are diagonal), the kernel

(\Delta\mathsf{i},\Delta\mathsf{k})\mapsto\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}(1-z)^{\Delta\mathsf{i}}z^{-\Delta\mathsf{k}-1}\mathrm{d}z

(5.26)

is called
the incomplete beta kernel

[
OR03
]
; in our case
\Delta\mathsf{i}=\mathsf{i}-\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\Delta\mathsf{k}=\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}
. When we restrict ourselves to the same slice
\mathsf{i}=\mathsf{i}^{\prime}
a simple integration shows it becomes
the discrete sine kernel

(\mathsf{k},\mathsf{k}^{\prime})\mapsto\frac{\sin(\theta(\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}))}{\pi(\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{k}^{\prime})}

(5.27)

where
\theta=\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
is the argument of
z_{+}=z_{+}(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
.

Theorem
5.6
justifies the word
arctic curve
for
\mathcal{L}
in the following sense: if one looks at a large system and scales appropriately, in the particle–hole description, above
\mathcal{C}
we will see only holes in the sense that the probability of seeing a particle decays exponentially; below
\mathcal{C}
we will see only particles — the probability of seeing particles is exponentially close to 1 that is, as can be verified from the explicit limit of the diagonal elements of the kernel
K(i,k;i,k)
. Inside
\mathcal{C}
we will see a mixture, and this region is called the
liquid region
— denoted above by
\mathcal{L}
. We illustrate this in Figure
10
.

Theorem
5.6
also gives us the limiting density of particles, given below. Similar results for various related models of plane partitions can be found in e.g.
[
CK01
, Section 2]
,
[
OR03
, Corollary 2]
,
[
Ken08
, Sections 4 and 8]
and
[
BF14
, Section 3]
.

Corollary 5.9
.

In the limit (
5.8
) and around macroscopic point
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
the density of particles is

\rho(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=\frac{\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})}{\pi},\qquad\theta(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=\arg(z_{+}(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})).

(5.28)

Note that, for
\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, the density is 0 above
\mathcal{C}
(as the two critical points are real so
\theta=0
), strictly between 0 and 1 inside
\mathcal{L}
and 1 below (as the two roots are negative below
\mathcal{C}
). For
\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}
, the density is 0 above
\mathcal{C}
, strictly between 0 and 1 when inside
\mathcal{L}
where the two roots are complex conjugate, 0 again (!) for
\mathpzc{y}
below
\mathcal{C}
but above the line
\mathpzc{x}=\mathpzc{a}+\mathpzc{y}
, and 1 below
\mathcal{C}
and below the aforementioned line. This can immediately be translated into the density of horizontal tiles in the plane partition picture via the change of variables
\mathpzc{y}=\mathpzc{h}+\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{2}
— here
\mathpzc{h}=\lim_{r\to 0+}hr
is the macroscopic ordinate/height for lozenges.

In the case
\mathpzc{a}\to\infty
(
\mathpzc{A}\to 0
) the arctic curve
\mathcal{C}
becomes particularly simple. It can be written as half the zero locus (the part
\mathpzc{x}>0
) of

\begin{split}(1+U+V)(1+U-V)(1-U+V)(1-U-V)=0,\qquad(U,V)=(e^{-\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{2}},e^{-\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{2}}).\end{split}

(5.29)

It is thus half the boundary of the amoeba of the polynomial
p(U,V)=1+U+V

[
KO07
,
KO06
,
KOS06
]
and can be recovered using the techniques in
[
KO07
]
, independent of the description as a Schur process. We recall that the amoeba of a polynomial
p(U,V)\in\mathbb{C}[U,V]
is the set

\displaystyle\{(\log|U|,\log|V|):(U,V)\in(\mathbb{C}\setminus 0)^{2},p(U,V)=0\}.

(5.30)

We make a small digression and address the three-dimensional nature of the picture. It is possible to recover the three-dimensional limit surface from the above formulas. This was first obtained by Cerf and Kenyon
[
CK01
]
in the mathematical literature and by Blöte, Hilhorst and Nienhuis in the physics literature
[
NHB84
]
, and while we can recover their formulas, as already noted in
[
OR03
]
(whose exposition we follow for this purpose), further analysis — in particular a concentration inequality type of result — is needed to make the computations rigorous (but see
[
CK01
]
where this is done using an alternative method). We also note that all the cited references deal with regular (non-symmetric) plane partitions, but the limit surface is the same after a mild reparametrization (so that indeed the symmetric plane partitions, as opposed to just the free boundary halves, are distributed according to the same
q^{\text{Volume}}
measure as the ordinary plane partitions). Here and below we consider
\rho
as a function of
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})
, and we extend to the symmetric case by
\rho(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})=\rho(-\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})
. We denote
X,Y,Z
the three-dimensional coordinates of space, with
Z
the vertical,
X
the south-west and
Y
the south-east coordinates respectively in the
(1,1,1)
projection. Then a parametrization of the limit surface, depicted in Figure
13
for the case of unbounded bottom, is:

Z(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})=\int_{-\infty}^{\mathpzc{h}}(1-\rho(\mathpzc{x},s))ds,\qquad X(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})=Z(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})-\mathpzc{h}-\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{2},\qquad Y(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})=Z(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{h})-\mathpzc{h}+\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{2}.

(5.31)

Figure 13
:
The limit surface for both symmetric and non-symmetric random plane partitions.

We now address, again in the case
\mathpzc{x}>0
, the limits of
K_{1,1}
and
K_{2,2}
. It will be easier to work with the conjugated kernel
\tilde{K}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})
defined thusly. We fix
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
and consider the corresponding
z_{+}
. Then (with
S(z_{+}):=S(z_{+};\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
):

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}\tilde{K}_{1,1}&\tilde{K}_{1,2}\\
\tilde{K}_{2,1}&\tilde{K}_{2,2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}e^{-\Re S(z_{+})/r}&0\\
0&e^{\Re S(z_{+})/r}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}K_{1,1}&K_{1,2}\\
K_{2,1}&K_{2,2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{rr}e^{-\Re S(z_{+})/r}&0\\
0&e^{\Re S(z_{+})/r}\end{array}\right).

It follows that diagonal elements of
\tilde{K}
converge to zero.

Theorem 5.10
.

With the same assumptions as in Theorem
5.6
, in the asymptotic regime given by (
5.8
) we find that

\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{1,1}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{2,2}(i,k;i^{\prime},k^{\prime})=0
.

Proof.

It turns out the analysis was already carried out in the proof of Theorem
5.6
, with one particular difference: now we can move both the
z
and the
w
contours around and deform them into one and the same contour, called
\gamma_{1,1}
for
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
and
\gamma_{2,2}
for
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
— we can do this as there is no more residue/singularity to take care of at
z=w
as the diagonal elements of the kernel do not contain a
(z-w)
term in the denominator of the integrand. In the frozen regions (
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+}
or
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
) the contours both pass through
z_{+}
, while in the liquid region (
\mathpzc{y}\in(\mathpzc{y}_{-},\mathpzc{y}_{+})
) both pass through
z_{+}
and
z_{-}=\overline{z_{+}}
respectively. In all three cases they intersect in the critical point(s) at right angles, and away from them
\gamma_{1,1}
follows a direction of descent, while
\gamma_{2,2}
one of ascent. In fact in all three cases,
\gamma_{1,1}
can be taken to be the final
z
contour from the proof of Theorem
5.6
, while
\gamma_{2,2}
the final
w
contour. See Figure
12
(top left, top right, bottom left):
\gamma_{1,1}=C_{z}
and
\gamma_{2,2}=C_{w}
.

More precisely, the integrands in
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
and
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
can be approximated by

\exp\frac{1}{r}\left(\tilde{S}(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})+\tilde{S}(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\right)\text{ and\ }\exp-\frac{1}{r}\left(\tilde{S}(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})+\tilde{S}(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})\right)

(5.40)

respectively as
r\to 0+
where
\tilde{S}(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})=S(z;\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})-\Re S(z_{+};\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
with the observation that throughout the proof the dependence on
\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y}
will be omitted.

The important remark is that
\tilde{S}
has real part zero at
z_{+}
(and in the liquid region, at
z_{-}
as well), and so we deform the two contours into one and the same such that for
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
the real part of
S(z)-S(z_{+})
decreases away from
z_{+}
(and so will become negative), while for
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
it increases away from
z_{+}
thus becoming positive. Then in both cases the integrals will converge to 0 as
r
goes to 0 using the basic asymptotic fact (
5.22
). We finally remark that for
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
deformation of the contours has to avoid the interval
(\frac{1}{\mathpzc{X}},\frac{1}{\mathpzc{A}})
, while for
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
we need to avoid the interval
(0,1)
— both of which can be achieved.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

\Re S(z_{+})
can be made more explicit as both
S
and
z_{+}
have explicit formulas, but we decided to avoid this to streamline the proof. In the case
\mathpzc{a}\to\infty
the argument (originally the one from
[
OR03
]
) simplifies considerably, and it will be presented, in a slightly modified form (i.e., for a different model) in Section
6
.

The discussion above, especially Theorems
5.6
and
5.10
and Remark
5.8
, allows us to formulate the main result as: “away from the arctic curve, for
\mathpzc{x}>0
, the local pfaffian correlations, in the limit, become determinantal with kernel given by the incomplete beta kernel.”

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
n>0
be a natural number,
\mathpzc{a}\in(0,\infty)
,
\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y}
reals with
\mathpzc{x}\in(0,\mathpzc{a}),\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{x}-\mathpzc{a}
, and for
1\leq s\leq n
fix
n
pairs
\mathsf{i}_{s}\in\mathbb{N},\mathsf{k}_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
. As
r\to 0+
assume we have
i_{s}\in\mathbb{N},k_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
depending on
r
and converging
ri_{s}\to\mathpzc{x}
,
rk_{s}\to\mathpzc{y}
thusly:

\displaystyle i_{s}=\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{x}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{i}_{s},\qquad k_{s}=\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k}_{s},

(5.41)

and denote
U=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\}.
Then in the asymptotic regime given by (
5.8
) we have

\displaystyle{\varrho}(U)\to\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{+}\text{ or }(\mathpzc{x}>\mathpzc{x}_{T}\text{ and }\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}),\\
\det_{1\leq u,v\leq n}B(\Delta\mathsf{i}_{uv},\Delta\mathsf{k}_{uv}),&\text{ if }\mathpzc{y}\in(\mathpzc{y}_{-},\mathpzc{y}_{+}),\\
1,&\text{ if }\mathpzc{x}<\mathpzc{x}_{T}\text{ and }\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}\end{cases}

(5.42)

where
\Delta\mathsf{i}_{uv}=\mathsf{i}_{u}-\mathsf{i}_{v},\Delta\mathsf{k}_{uv}=\mathsf{k}_{u}-\mathsf{k}_{v}
,
B(\Delta\mathsf{i},\Delta\mathsf{k})
is the incomplete beta kernel

\displaystyle(\Delta\mathsf{i},\Delta\mathsf{k})\mapsto\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}(1-z)^{\Delta\mathsf{i}}z^{-\Delta\mathsf{k}-1}\mathrm{d}z

(5.43)

with
\gamma_{\pm}
connecting the two critical points
z_{\pm}
,
\gamma_{+}
passing to the right of 0 (and
\gamma_{-}
passing to the left), with
\gamma_{+}
being chosen in the case
\mathsf{i}_{v}\leq\mathsf{i}_{u}
(and
\gamma_{-}
otherwise).

In the case
\mathpzc{x}=0
(
\mathpzc{X}=1
), the limiting regime
r\to 0+
for the local microscopic coordinates becomes
(i,k)

i=\mathsf{i},\qquad k=\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k}

(5.44)

which for
i
is of course a relabeling we nevertheless use to be consistent with our notation so far. Because
\mathpzc{y}_{+}=\infty
, the coordinate
\mathpzc{y}
is either in the liquid region (
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{-}=-\log\frac{4}{(1+\mathpzc{A})^{2}}
) in which case the double critical points are complex conjugate of modulus 1, or in the frozen region (
-\mathpzc{a}<\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
) in which case the double critical points are real negative. Moreover, on the circle
\{|z|=1\}
(of interest in the liquid region) the real part of
S
is constant and equal to 0:

\displaystyle 2\Re S(z)=S(z)+S(1/z)=0.

(5.45)

with values increasing (positive) and decreasing (negative) outside (remark that in the notation from above
C_{0}=\{|z|=1\}
).

When one passes the contours through the critical points, the preceding arguments work almost unchanged. The few observations we make are as follows.

First, the contours for
z
and
w
exist even when
\mathpzc{x}=0
since for any finite
q=\exp(-r)
there is enough space to the right of 1 between the largest zero of
F
(
=q<1
) and its smallest pole (
=q^{-\mathsf{i}-1}>1
). Thus, even though we have a pole at
1
in the integrand for
K_{1,2}
and
K_{2,2}
, this will never give a residual contribution as the contours can avoid passing through 1.

Second, in both cases —
\mathpzc{y}
inside/outside the liquid region, we have to exchange the two contours (and if
\mathpzc{y}
is inside the liquid region, both have to pass through
z_{+}=\overline{z_{-}}
), in which case we will pick up the contributions from the residues along the hyperbola
zw=1
in the analysis of
K_{1,1}
and
K_{2,2}
. Their contributions are, in the limit
r\to 0+
:

\begin{split}Res_{z\to\frac{1}{w}}K_{1,1}&\to\int_{\gamma_{+}}(1-w)^{\mathsf{i}}\left(1-\frac{1}{w}\right)^{\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}w^{\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-1}\frac{1-w}{1+w}\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}},\\
Res_{z\to\frac{1}{w}}K_{2,2}&\to\int_{-\gamma_{-}}(1-w)^{-\mathsf{i}}\left(1-\frac{1}{w}\right)^{-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}w^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\frac{w+1}{w-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\end{split}

(5.46)

where
\gamma_{+}
is a closed contour passing through
z_{+}<0
and to the right of 1 if
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
or otherwise joins the two complex conjugate critical points (again to the right of 1) if
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{-}
. The minus sign in front of
\gamma_{-}
in the second integral can be brought inside the integral and appears due to the fact
\gamma_{-}
is oriented bottom-to-top, while our
z
and
w
contours are counterclockwise.

Third,
\tilde{K}=K
if
\mathpzc{y}>\mathpzc{y}_{-}
as the real part of
S
at the critical points is 0.

In view of the above, we have shown the correlations remain pfaffian when
\mathpzc{x}=0
with a kernel we have explicitly computed. We formally state the result.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
n>0
be a natural number,
\mathpzc{a}\in(0,\infty)
,
\mathpzc{y}>-\mathpzc{a}
real, and for
1\leq s\leq n
fix
n
pairs
\mathsf{i}_{s}\in\mathbb{N},\mathsf{k}_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
. As
r\to 0+
assume we have
i_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
, as well as
k_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\prime}
depending on
r
converging thusly:

i_{s}=\mathsf{i}_{s},\qquad k_{s}=\left\lfloor\frac{\mathpzc{y}}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k}_{s}.

(5.47)

Denote
U=\{(i_{1},j_{1}),\dots,(i_{n},j_{n})\}.
Then in the asymptotic regime given by (
5.8
) the correlations
{\varrho}(U)
converge to
1
if
\mathpzc{y}<\mathpzc{y}_{-}
and are otherwise pfaffian with
2\times 2
matrix kernel given by:

\begin{split}\mathsf{K}_{1,1}(\mathsf{i},\mathsf{k};\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}^{\prime})&=\int_{\gamma_{+}}(1-z)^{\mathsf{i}}\left(1-\frac{1}{z}\right)^{\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}z^{\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-1}\frac{1-z}{1+z}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}},\\
\mathsf{K}_{1,2}(\mathsf{i},\mathsf{k};\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}^{\prime})&=\int_{\gamma_{\pm}}(1-z)^{\mathsf{i}-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}z^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}},\\
\mathsf{K}_{2,2}(\mathsf{i},\mathsf{k};\mathsf{i}^{\prime},\mathsf{k}^{\prime})&=\int_{\gamma_{-}}(1-z)^{-\mathsf{i}}\left(1-\frac{1}{z}\right)^{-\mathsf{i}^{\prime}}z^{\mathsf{k}^{\prime}-\mathsf{k}-1}\frac{1+z}{1-z}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\end{split}

(5.48)

where
\gamma_{\pm}
are as in Theorem
5.12
and
\gamma_{+}
is taken if and only if
\mathsf{i}^{\prime}\leq\mathsf{i}
.

Remark 5.14
.

It is crucial that we keep
i_{s}=\mathsf{i}_{s}
finite as
r\to 0+
to obtain a pfaffian process. If instead we rescale

i_{s}=f(r)+\mathsf{i}_{s}
with
1\ll f(r)\ll r^{-1}
, then we obtain a determinantal process with the kernel
\mathsf{K}_{1,2}
. To see this, first observe that under this rescaling, by deforming the contours as before, we may still reduce the double contour integral representation for

\mathsf{K}_{1,2}
(resp.
\mathsf{K}_{1,1}
and
\mathsf{K}_{2,2}
) to a single integral of the residue of the integrand at
z=w
(resp. at
zw=1
). The expression for the residue involves some
q
-Pochhammer symbols which may be estimated using (
B.7
) — namely
(z;q)_{i}=(1-z)^{i}e^{o(i)}
for
i\ll r^{-1}
.
For
\mathsf{K}_{1,2}
, the residue has the same finite limit as before, since the dependency on
f(r)
disappears in the ratio
\frac{F(N-i,w)}{F(N-i^{\prime},w)}
. But this is not the case for
\mathsf{K}_{1,1}
and
\mathsf{K}_{2,2}
which involve respectively the product
F(N-i,w)F(N-i^{\prime},w)
and its inverse. Instead, we find that the dominant contribution to the integral comes from the endpoints,
since
|1-z|
is maximal on
\gamma_{+}
and minimal on
\gamma_{-}
at the endpoints. Setting
R(i,z)=(z;q)_{i}
, we conclude that
R^{-1}(i,z_{+})R^{-1}(j,z_{+}){K}_{1,1}
and
R(i,z_{+})R(j,z_{+}){K}_{2,2}
both converge to zero. Using the conjugation trick of Proposition
A.1
, the correlation functions are found to be determinantal in the limit.

6
Plane overpartitions

A
plane overpartition
is a plane partition where in each row the last occurrence of an integer can be overlined or not while all the other occurrences of this integer are not overlined, and in each column the first occurrence of an integer can be overlined or not while all the other occurrences of this integer are overlined. A plane overpartition with the largest entry at most
N
and shape
\lambda
can be recorded as a sequence of partitions

\emptyset\prec\lambda^{(1)}\prec^{\prime}\lambda^{(2)}\prec\cdots\prec\lambda^{(2n-1)}\prec^{\prime}\lambda^{(2N)}=\lambda

where
\lambda^{(i)}
is the partition whose shape is formed by all fillings greater than
{N-i/2}
, where the convention is that
\overline{k}=k-1/2
. An example of a plane overpartition is given in Figure
14
.

1
\overline{3}
3
4
\overline{1}
3
\overline{4}
\overline{3}
\overline{3}
\overline{2}
2
2

Figure 14
:
A plane overpartition
\emptyset\prec(1)\prec^{\prime}(2)\prec(2,2)\prec^{\prime}(3,3,1)\prec(5,3,1)\prec^{\prime}(5,4,1)\prec(5,4,1,1)\prec^{\prime}(5,4,2,1)
.

A plane partition
\pi
is called a (diagonally) strict plane partition if its diagonals
\pi^{(t)}=(\pi_{i,i+t})_{i\geq 1}
are strict partitions, i.e. strictly decreasing sequences of integers. By deleting the overlines in a plane overpartition one obtains a strict plane partition. Conversely, a strict plane partition can be overlined to obtain a plane overpartition and there are
2^{\#\textrm{border components}}
different ways to do it. A border component of a strict plane partitions is a set of rookwise-connected boxes (i.e., a connected ribbon/border strip) filled with the same number. The strict plane partition obtained by deleting the overlines in Figure
14
has five border components.

A measure that to a plane overpartition with the largest entry at most
N
assigns a weight
q^{|\textrm{sum of all entries}|}
is an HV-ascending Schur process with
t=1
and
x_{1}=x_{2}=q^{N},\dots,x_{2N-1}=x_{2N}=q^{1}
.

The asymptotics of strict plane partitions was studied in
[
Vul07
]
using a variant of the original Schur process, where in the definition of the process the skew Schur
P
and
Q
functions were used instead of the standard Schur symmetric functions. There a strict plane partition is represented by a finite point configuration where each point in the configuration corresponds to one entry in the strict plane partition. Precisely, a strict partition can be represented by the set of its parts, since all parts are different, and hence a strict plane partition
\pi
can be represented by a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, where
(t,x)
belongs to it if
x
is a part of
\pi^{(t)}
. See Figure
15
(left). The set of blue points is the point configuration corresponding to the strict plane partition obtained by deleting the overlines in Figure
14
.

The above point configuration is not a suitable representation of a plane overpartition, since there are
2^{\#\textrm{border components}}
different ways to overline a strict plane partition. We need a new set of point configurations to represent these different overlinings. We explain briefly how these point configurations are obtained and refer the reader to
[
BCC17
]
and
[
CSV11
]
for details and proofs.

Starting from the point configuration of the corresponding strict plane partition we first construct a set of nonintersecting paths, where each path corresponds to a row of the plane partition. Going from
\pi_{i,j}
to
\pi_{i,j+1}
we take an eastbound edge followed by
\pi_{i,j}-\pi_{i,j+1}
southbound edges if
\pi_{i,j}
is not overlined and a southeastbound edge followed by
\pi_{i,j}-\pi_{i,j+1}-1
southbound edges if
\pi_{i,j}
is overlined. See Figure
15
(left). Once we have the nonintersecting paths we can produce a domino tiling. Dominos are placed diagonally and cover
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. It is possible to place them in such a way so that different types of dominos (divided by the direction of the position and the color of the top corner in a chessboard fashion coloring) correspond to different edges. See Figure
15
(right). Finally, place two particles (holes) at the center of each of the two domino squares if the top corner of that domino is black (white). The point configuration is the set of all particles. See Figure
15
(right), where particles are shown in solid colors. It corresponds to the example from Figure
14
.

\pi^{(-4)}
\pi^{(-1)}
\pi^{(0)}
\pi^{(1)}
\pi^{(2)}
\pi^{(5)}
\cdots
\cdots
t
x
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet
\emptyset

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bullet

Figure 15
:
Strict plane partition point configuration (left) and plane overpartition point configuration (right).

One can show that a point configuration representing a plane overpartition

\emptyset\prec\lambda^{(1)}\prec^{\prime}\lambda^{(2)}\prec\cdots\prec\lambda^{(2n-1)}\prec^{\prime}\lambda^{(2N)}=\lambda

is a subset of
\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\cup\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}\right)
consisting of points
(t,x)
such that the Maya diagram of
\lambda^{i(x)}
contains a particle at the position
\overline{t}
:
\overline{t}=\lambda^{i(x)}_{j}-j+1/2
, for some
j\geq 1
with
i(x)=2N-2(x-1/4)
and

\overline{t}=\lceil{t-1/2}\rceil-1/2.

(6.1)

For the HV-ascending Schur process with
x_{1}=x_{2}=q^{N},\dots,x_{2N-1}=x_{2N}=q^{1}
:

F_{HV}(i,z)=\frac{(-q^{N+1-\lfloor{i/2}\rfloor}z;q)_{\lfloor{i/2}\rfloor}}{(q^{N+1-\lceil{i/2}\rceil}z;q)_{\lceil{i/2}\rceil}}\frac{(q/z;q)_{N}}{(-q/z;q)_{N}},\quad i=1,\dots,2N.

(6.2)

If we set
F(x,z)=F_{HV}(i(x),z)
then

F(x,z)=\frac{(-q^{\lceil x+1/2\rceil}z;q)_{N+1-{\lceil x+1/2\rceil}}}{(q^{\lceil x\rceil}z;q)_{N+1-{\lceil x\rceil}}}\frac{(q/z;q)_{N}}{(-q/z;q)_{N}}
and when
N\to\infty

F(x,z)=\frac{(-q^{\lceil x+1/2\rceil}z;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{\lceil x\rceil}z;q)_{\infty}}\frac{(q/z;q)_{\infty}}{(-q/z;q)_{\infty}}.

(6.3)

Remark 6.1
.

For
q\in(0,1)
and fixed
x
, zeros of
F
as a function of
z
are
-q^{-\lceil x+1/2\rceil},-q^{-\lceil x+1/2\rceil-1},\dots
, which belong to
(-\infty,-q^{-\lceil{x+1/2}\rceil}]
, and
q,q^{2},\dots
, which belong to
(0,q]
. Poles are
q^{-\lceil x\rceil},q^{-\lceil x\rceil-1},\dots
, which belong to
[q^{-\lceil{x}\rceil},\infty)
, and
-q,-q^{2},\dots
, which belong to
[-q,0)
.

In our asymptotic analysis we will consider
q\in(0,1)
and
x_{i}
such that
rx_{i}\to\chi
when
r\to 0+
where
q=e^{-{r}}
. We will need to avoid zeros or poles of
F(x_{i},z)
along certain contours in
z
for
r
close to
0+
. Zeros, respectively poles, could be avoided if we make sure to choose contours that do not cut
\mathcal{Z}(\chi)=(-\infty,-e^{\chi}]\cup(0,1]
, respectively
\mathcal{P}(\chi)=[-1,0)\cup[e^{\chi},\infty).

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
(t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n})\in\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\cup\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}\right)
. Pfaffian correlations are given by the following matrix kernel:

\begin{split}K(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}K_{1,1}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})&K_{1,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})\\
-K_{1,2}(t_{j},x_{j};t_{i},x_{i})&K_{2,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})\end{array}\right]\end{split}

(6.4)

where

\begin{split}K_{1,1}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})&=[z^{\overline{t_{i}}}w^{\overline{t_{j}}}]F(x_{i},z)F(x_{j},w)\frac{\sqrt{zw}(z-w)}{(z+1)(w+1)(zw-1)},\quad 1<|w|,1<|z|,\\
K_{1,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})&=\left[\frac{z^{\overline{t_{i}}}}{w^{\overline{t_{j}}}}\right]\frac{F(x_{i},z)}{F(x_{j},w)}\frac{\sqrt{zw}(zw-1)}{(z-w)(z+1)(w-1)},\quad 1<|z|,1<|w|,|w|{\begin{subarray}{c}<\\
>\end{subarray}}|z|,\textrm{ for }x_{i}{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq\\
<\end{subarray}}x_{j},\\
K_{2,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})&=\left[\frac{1}{z^{\overline{t_{i}}}w^{\overline{t_{j}}}}\right]\frac{1}{F(x_{i},z)F(x_{j},w)}\frac{\sqrt{zw}(z-w)}{(z-1)(w-1)(zw-1)},\quad 1<|z|,1<|w|,\end{split}

(6.5)

and
\overline{t}
is as in (
6.1
).

Remark 6.3
.

Let
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
. Define

\tilde{K}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{\chi\overline{t_{i}}/2}&0\\
0&e^{-\chi\overline{t_{i}}/2}\end{matrix}\right){K}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})\left(\begin{matrix}e^{\chi\overline{t_{j}}/2}&0\\
0&e^{-\chi\overline{t_{j}}/2}\end{matrix}\right).

(6.6)

Then

\pf[\tilde{K}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\pf[K(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}.

(6.7)

Let

G(x,t,z)=r\left(\log F(x,z)-\overline{t}(\log z-\chi/2)\right).

(6.8)

Using Theorem
6.2

\tilde{K}_{1,1}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}\frac{z-w}{\sqrt{zw}(z+1)(w+1)(zw-1)}\exp\left[\frac{1}{r}\left(G(\overline{t_{i}},x_{i},z)+G(\overline{t_{j}},x_{j},w)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w

(6.9)

where
C_{z}
and
C_{w}
are simple closed counterclockwise oriented contours such that
|z|>1
,
z\notin\mathcal{P}(\chi)
,
|w|>1
, and
w\notin\mathcal{P}(\chi)
for all
z\in C_{z}
and
w\in C_{w}
;

\tilde{K}_{1,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}\frac{zw-1}{\sqrt{zw}(z-w)(z+1)(w-1)}\exp\left[\frac{1}{r}\left(G(\overline{t_{i}},x_{i},z)-G(\overline{t_{j}},x_{j},w)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w

(6.10)

where
C_{z}
and
C_{w}
are simple closed counterclockwise oriented contours such that
|z|>1
,
z\notin\mathcal{P}(\chi)
,
|w|>1
,
w\notin\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
,
|z|>|w|
(
|z|<|w|
), for all
z\in C_{z}
and
w\in C_{w}
when
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
(
x_{i}<x_{j}
);

\tilde{K}_{2,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{1}{(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{2}}\int_{C_{z}}\int_{C_{w}}\frac{z-w}{\sqrt{zw}(z-1)(w-1)(zw-1)}\exp\left[\frac{1}{r}\left(-G(\overline{t_{i}},x_{i},z)-G(\overline{t_{j}},x_{j},w)\right)\right]\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}w

(6.11)

where
C_{z}
and
C_{w}
are two simple closed counterclockwise oriented contours such that
|z|>1
,
z\notin\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
,
|w|>1
,
w\notin\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
for all
z\in C_{z}
and
w\in C_{w}
.

Before we proceed with the asymptotics, we set up some notation.

Define
C^{+}(R,\theta)
(
C^{-}(R,\theta)
) to be the counterclockwise (clockwise) oriented arc on
|z|=R
from
Re^{-i\theta}
to
Re^{i\theta}
for
R>0
and
0\leq\theta\leq\pi
. The counterclockwise oriented circle
|z|=R
is then
C^{+}(R,\theta)\cup-C^{-}(R,\theta)
.

For a sequence of real numbers
a_{i}
we define an (infinite) integer matrix

{\Delta}(a)_{ij}=\lceil a_{i}\rceil-\lceil a_{j}\rceil.

(6.12)

Let
\mathcal{D}=\left\{(\tau,\chi)|\tau\in\mathbb{R},\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},-1\leq f(\tau,\chi)\leq 1\right\}=\left\{(\tau,\chi)|\chi\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},-\tau_{c}(\chi)\leq\tau\leq\tau_{c}(\chi)\right\}

where

f(\tau,\chi)=\frac{(e^{\chi}+1)(e^{\tau}-1)}{2e^{\chi/2}(e^{\tau}+1)},\quad\tau_{c}(\chi)=2\log\frac{e^{\chi/2}+1}{e^{\chi/2}-1}.

(6.13)

Note that
\mathcal{D}
is a domain bounded by three curves:
\chi=0
and
-1\pm e^{\chi/2}-e^{\tau/2}\mp e^{\tau/2}e^{\chi/2}=0
for
\chi>0.
Amoeba of
P(z,w)=-1+z+w+zw
is a domain bounded by the following four curves:

-1\pm e^{\omega}-e^{\xi}\mp e^{\xi}e^{\omega}=0,\quad\omega>0,\text{\ and\ }-1\pm e^{\omega}+e^{\xi}\pm e^{\xi}e^{\omega}=0,\quad\omega<0.

(6.14)

If we set
(\xi,\omega)=(\tau/2,\chi/2)
then
\mathcal{D}
is the half of the amoeba of
-1+z+w+zw
for
\omega\geq 0
. Let

\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)=\begin{cases}\arccos(f(\tau,\chi)),&-\tau_{c}(\chi)\leq\tau\leq\tau_{c}(\chi),\\
0&\tau>\tau_{c}(\chi),\\
\pi&\tau<-\tau_{c}(\chi).\end{cases}

(6.15)

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
rt_{i},\,rt_{j}\to\tau
,
rx_{i},\,rx_{j}\to\chi
when
r\to 0+
where
q=e^{-r}
. Assume
\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}
,
\Delta(x)_{ij}
,
\Delta(x+{1/2})_{ij}
do not change with
r
. Then

\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{1,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{e^{-\frac{\chi}{2}\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{\pm}(e^{-\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))}\frac{1}{w^{\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}+1}}\frac{(1-w)^{\Delta(x)_{ij}}}{(1+w)^{\Delta(x+1/2)_{ij}}}\mathrm{d}w

(6.16)

where we choose
C^{+}(e^{-\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))
if
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
and
C^{-}(e^{-\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))
otherwise and where
\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)
is as in (
6.15
).

Proof.

We start with (
6.10
). The asymptotics is determined by the limit of
G
when
r\to 0+
:

S(z;\tau,\chi)=-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-e^{-\chi}z)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\frac{1}{z})+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(e^{-\chi}z)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\frac{1}{z})-\tau(\log z-\chi/2)

(6.17)

where
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)
is defined in Appendix
B
. Ignoring the
\log
part of
S
, the function is analytic on
\mathbb{C}\backslash{\mathcal{P}(\chi)}
.
We can easily compute

z\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}S(z;\tau,\chi)=-\tau+\log\frac{(1+e^{-\chi}z)(z+1)}{(1-e^{-\chi}z)(z-1)}

(6.18)

where by the Cauchy–Riemann equations

z\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}S(z;\tau,\chi)=x\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Re S+y\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\Re S+\mathrm{i}\left(y\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\Re S-x\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\Re S\right).

(6.19)

The real part of
S(z;\tau,\chi)
vanishes on the circle
z=e^{\chi/2}e^{i\theta}
. This implies that on this circle the imaginary part of
z\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}S(z;\tau,\chi)
vanishes too, which is the derivative of
\Re S
in the direction of the tangent, while the real part is equal to
R\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}R}\Re S(z;\tau,\chi)
. Then for
z=e^{\chi/2}e^{i\theta}

R\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}R}\Re S(z;\tau,\chi)=-\tau+\log\left|\frac{z+1}{z-1}\right|^{2},

(6.20)

which is negative if and only if
\cos\theta<f(\tau,\chi)
.

If
(\tau,\chi)\in\mathcal{D}
and
z=e^{\chi/2}e^{i\theta}
then
R\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}R}\Re S(z;\tau,\chi)
changes the sign along
|z|=e^{\chi/2}
at
\theta=\pm\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)
being positive for
|\theta|<|\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)|
. We then deform contours so that the real parts of
S(z,\tau,\chi)
and
S(w,\tau,\chi)
are negative everywhere on the new contours except at the critical points. The new contours look like
\gamma_{z}
and
\gamma_{w}
in Figure
16
. The integrals over these new contours vanish as
r\to 0+
, but we pick up the residue at
z=w
on
C^{+}(e^{\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))
(
C^{-}(e^{\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)
) when
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
(
x_{i}<x_{j}
). The residue is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}e^{\frac{\chi}{2}\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}}\int_{C^{\pm}(e^{\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))}\frac{1}{w^{\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}+1}}\frac{(1-e^{-\chi}w)^{\Delta(x)_{ij}}}{(1+e^{-\chi}w)^{\Delta(x+1/2)_{ij}}}\mathrm{d}w.

(6.21)

The change of variables
w\mapsto e^{-\chi}w
brings the expression from the statement of the theorem.

\gamma_{z}
\gamma_{w}
-1
1
e^{\chi}
-e^{\chi}
\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
\mathcal{P}(\chi)
\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
\mathcal{P}(\chi)
|z|=e^{\chi/2}

Figure 16
:
Deformed contours in
z
and
w
.

Some special care is needed when
\chi=0
because we are deforming the contours in a neighborhood of
|z|=1
and
|w|=1
. In this case
(\tau,\chi)\in\mathcal{D}
for all
\tau
. Observe that if we deform the contours as described above, the new contour in
z
, respectively
w
, will still encompass
z=-1
, respectively
w=1
, and we will not be picking up any residues coming from
z=-1
or
w=1
. Also note that while deforming the contours we remain away from
\mathcal{P}(\chi)
for the
z
-contour and
\mathcal{Z}(\chi)
for the
w
-contour.

Lastly, the integral
1/(z-w)
converges at the points of intersection of the contours. We can for simplicity make them cross orthogonally; then the integral of
1/(z-w)
along these contours is absolutely bounded by the convergent integral
\iint 1/\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}dxdy
.

If
(\tau,\chi)
does not belong to
\mathcal{D}
then
R\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}R}\Re S(z;\tau,\chi)
does not change sign. It is strictly positive for
\tau<0
and strictly negative for
\tau>0
. We just need to push the contour
C_{z}
inside (for
\tau<0
) or outside (for
\tau>0
) of
|z|=e^{\chi/2}
and push
C_{w}
outside (for
\tau<0
) or inside (for
\tau>0
) of
|w|=e^{\chi/2}
to obtain that both
\Re S(z;\tau,\chi)
and
\Re S(w;\tau,\chi)
are negative along the new contours. Then when
\tau>0
we get that
K_{1,2}
vanishes in the limit and when
\tau<0
we pick up the residue at
z=w
along the whole circle
|z|=e^{\chi/2}
.

∎

Remark 6.5
.

The density function of a particle present at
(\tau,\chi)
is

\rho(\tau,\chi)=\lim_{r\to 0+}K_{1,2}(t,x;t,x),\quad\textrm{for }rt\to\tau\textrm{ and }rx\to\chi.

(6.22)

From the theorem above

\rho(\tau,\chi)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{+}(e^{-\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))}\frac{1}{z}\mathrm{d}z=\frac{\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)}{\pi}.

(6.23)

A three-dimensional depiction of a plane overpartition
\pi
, obtained by stacking
\pi_{i,j}
unit cubes above the
(i,j)
-th position, is deciphered from the corresponding point configuration in the following way. Each particle
(t,x)
in the configuration is mapped to
(X,Y,Z)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
where
X
is the number of particles to the right of
(t,x)
counting the particle itself,
Y
is the number of holes to the left of
(t,x)
, or equivalently
X+\lfloor{t-1/2}\rfloor
, and
Z
is equal to
\lceil x+1/2\rceil
.
The limit shape of strict plane partitions is:

X(\tau,\chi)=\int_{\tau}^{\tau_{c}(\chi)}\rho(t,\chi)\mathrm{d}t,\quad Y(\tau,\chi)=\int_{-\tau_{c}(\chi)}^{\tau}(1-\rho(t,\chi))\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\tau}^{\tau_{c}(\chi)}\rho(t,\chi)\mathrm{d}t+\tau,\quad Z(\tau,\chi)=\chi

(6.24)

where
\tau_{c}(\chi)
is given by (
6.13
).
The two formulas for
Y
are indeed equal from the properties of the model, but this can be confirmed directly since

\int_{-\tau_{c}(\chi)}^{0}(1-\rho(t,\chi))\mathrm{d}t=\int_{-\tau_{c}(\chi)}^{0}\frac{\pi-\arccos{f(t,\chi)}}{\pi}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{-\tau_{c}(\chi)}^{0}\frac{\arccos{f(-t,\chi)}}{\pi}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\tau_{c}(\chi)}\rho(t,\chi)\mathrm{d}t.

(6.25)

Figure 17
:
A large random plane overpartition shown as a strict plane partition.

An exactly sampled large plane overpartition for
q=0.9
is shown in Figure
17
as a strict plane partition and as a domino tiling in Figure
6
. The simulations were carried out using the algorithms of
[
BBB+18
]
.

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
6.4
, diagonal elements of
\tilde{K}
vanish for
\chi>0
.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
rt_{i},\,rt_{j}\to\tau
,
rx_{i},\,rx_{j}\to\chi
when
r\to 0+
where
q=e^{-r}
. Assume
\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}
,
\Delta(x)_{ij}
,
\Delta(x+{1/2})_{ij}
do not change with
r
. Then for
\chi>0

\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{1,1}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{2,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=0.

(6.26)

When
\chi=0
assume in addition that
x_{i}
does not change with
r
. Set
e_{i}=\lceil x_{i}\rceil-\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{1,1}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{+}(1,\theta_{c}(\tau,0))}\frac{(-1)^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil-1}}{w^{-\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}+e_{i}+1}}\frac{(1-w)^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil+\lceil x_{j}\rceil-1}}{(1+w)^{\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil+\lceil x_{j}+1/2\rceil-1}}\mathrm{d}w,

(6.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0+}\tilde{K}_{2,2}(t_{i},x_{i};t_{j},x_{j})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{-}(1,\theta_{c}(\tau,0))}\frac{(-1)^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil-1}}{w^{\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}-e_{i}+1}}\frac{(1-w)^{1-\lceil x_{i}\rceil-\lceil x_{j}\rceil}}{(1+w)^{1-\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil-\lceil x_{j}+1/2\rceil}}\mathrm{d}w.

(6.28)

Proof.

We start with (
6.9
). The analysis was done in the proof of Theorem
6.4
. We can conclude that we can deform
C_{z}
and
C_{w}
into new contours such that the real parts of
S(z,\tau,\chi)
and
S(w,\tau,\chi)
are negative everywhere on the new contours except at the critical points. We can choose to deform them to the same contour
\gamma_{z}
in Figure
16
. When
\chi>0
there is no residue to pick up since while deforming the contours we keep
|z|>1
,
z\notin\mathcal{P}(\chi)
,
|w|>1
and
z\notin\mathcal{P}(\chi)
. The proof is analogous for
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
.

In the case where
\chi=0
we can proceed as above, but now for
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
we will be picking up the residue at
z=1/w
along
C^{+}(1,\theta_{c}(\tau,0))
for
\tilde{K}_{1,1}
and along
-C^{-}(1,\theta_{c}(\tau,0))
for
\tilde{K}_{2,2}
. Computing the residues we get (
6.27
) and (
6.28
).
∎

Let
(\tau,\chi)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},t,x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
. Define

\begin{split}B_{\tau,\chi}^{\pm}(t,x,y)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{C^{\pm}(e^{-\chi/2},\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi))}\frac{1}{z^{t+1}}\frac{(1-z)^{x}}{(1+z)^{y}}\mathrm{d}z\end{split}

(6.29)

where
\theta_{c}(\tau,\chi)
is as in (
6.15
).

From Theorems
6.4
and
6.6
we obtain that in the limit the bulk has determinantal correlations. Precisely, after factoring out constants that cancel out in the pfaffian as in Remark
6.3
, we get the following.

Theorem 6.7
.

For
i=1,\dots,n
let
rt_{i}\to\tau
,
rx_{i}\to\chi>0
when
r\to 0+
where
q=e^{-r}
. Assume
\Delta(t-1/2)
,
\Delta(x)
,
\Delta(x+{1/2})
do not change with
r
. Then

\lim_{r\to 0+}\varrho((t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n}))=\det\left[B^{\tau,\chi}_{\pm}\left(\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij},\Delta(x)_{ij},\Delta(x+1/2)_{ij}\right)\right]_{i,j=1,\dots,n}

(6.30)

where we choose
+
if
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
and
-
otherwise.

At the boundary
\chi=0
limit correlations remain pfaffian.

Theorem 6.8
.

For
i=1,\dots,n
and
q=e^{-r}
let
t_{i}
and
x_{i}
be such that
rt_{i}\to\tau
when
r\to 0+
, and
\Delta(t-1/2)
, and
x_{i}
do not change with
r
. Then

\lim_{r\to 0+}\varrho((t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n}))=\pf\left[\begin{matrix}B_{1,1}(i,j)&B_{1,2}(i,j)\\
-B_{1,2}(j,i)&B_{2,2}(i,j)\end{matrix}\right]_{1\leq i,j\leq n}

(6.31)

where

\begin{split}B_{1,1}(i,j)&=(-1)^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil-1}B_{\tau,0}^{+}\left(-\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}+e_{i},\lceil x_{i}\rceil+\lceil{x_{j}}\rceil-1,\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil+\lceil{x_{j}+1/2}\rceil-1\right),\\
B_{1,2}(i,j)&=B_{\tau,0}^{\pm}\left(\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij},\Delta(x)_{ij},\Delta(x+1/2)_{ij}\right),\\
B_{2,2}(i,j)&=(-1)^{\lceil x_{i}\rceil-1}B_{\tau,0}^{-}\left(\Delta(t-1/2)_{ij}-e_{i},1-\lceil x_{i}\rceil-\lceil{x_{j}}\rceil,1-\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil-\lceil{x_{j}+1/2}\rceil\right)\end{split}

(6.32)

where
e_{i}=\lceil x_{i}\rceil-\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil
and we choose
+
if
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
and
-
otherwise.

Remark 6.9
.

If in Theorem
6.8
instead of keeping
x_{i}
constant we let
x_{i}\to\infty
, while still keeping
rx_{i}\to 0
, we get that the correlations in the limit become determinantal with the kernel
B_{1,2}(i,j)
. To show this one can adapt the proof of Theorem
6.6
where, as before, we deform the contours and end up with the residue at
z=1/w
along
C^{\pm}(1,\theta_{c}(\tau,0))
. We can then use the fact that
(z;q)_{x}=(1-z)^{x}e^{o(x)}
when
x\to\infty
and
rx\to 0
and that
|(1-z)/(1+z)|
achieves its maximum (minimum) on
C^{+}
(
C^{-}
) at the endpoints to show that
R(i,z_{c})R(j,z_{c})\tilde{K}_{1,1}
and
R^{-1}(i,z_{c})R^{-1}(j,z_{c})\tilde{K}_{2,2}
both vanish, where the conjugation factor
R(i,z)
is
(z;q)_{x_{i}}/(-z;q)_{x_{i}}
and
z_{c}
is one of the endpoints
z_{c}=e^{i\theta_{c}(\tau,0)}
. See also Remark
5.14
.

Remark 6.10
.

Theorem
2.13
follows from Theorems
6.7
and
6.8
if we only consider the point process on points
(t_{1},x_{1}),\dots,(t_{n},x_{n})\in\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\times\left(\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{4}\right)
, in which case
\lceil x_{i}\rceil=\lceil x_{i}+1/2\rceil
. To obtain Theorem
2.13
we need to rescale our microscopic coordinates
(t,x)
as

t=\left\lfloor\frac{\tau}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{k},\qquad x=\left\lfloor\frac{\chi}{r}\right\rfloor+\mathsf{i}+\frac{1}{4}

(6.33)

and note that there
(\mathpzc{x},\mathpzc{y})
and
\mathcal{L}
were used instead of
(\tau,\chi)
and
\mathcal{D}
.

7
Conclusion

In this paper we have introduced the Schur process with two free boundary partitions; computed its pfaffian correlations upon charge mixing; and provided a uniform asymptotic treatment, upon killing one of the free boundaries, of various combinatorial and statistical mechanical models: symmetric last passage percolation, symmetric plane partitions and plane overpartitions.

We mention a few directions that merit further investigation: asymptotic analysis of the full two free boundary correlations, where we expect new kernels and “universality” behavior to appear — we plan to address this in a future work; possible generalizations to Hall–Littlewood,
q
-Whittaker, or even Macdonald processes — Fock space does not quite exist here so new ideas in the spirit of
[
BBCW18
]
are needed; analysis of other combinatorial models that
can
be treated using one free boundary — see
[
Rai00
, Section 7]
for a flavor; and possible connections to matrix models,
\tau
-functions, enumerative algebraic geometry and map counting.
